I guess… mostly my mom wanted me to come, but I know something’s wrong too. I just can’t seem to make myself do anything anymore. Like I sit in my room for hours, sometimes most of the day, and I’ll still be in the same clothes, even if I know I should change or shower or at least do something.

I used to have little things I did, like knitting, journaling, going for walks, and it wasn’t like my whole life was amazing or anything, but I had things. Now I just… don’t. I think about doing them and then it feels like there’s this huge wall between me and actually moving. My mom asks me what’s wrong or tries to get me to talk, and I barely answer because even answering feels like too much effort. It’s embarrassing, honestly. Yeah… definitely more than usual. I mean, it’s gotten really bad, honestly. I’ll wake up and know I should shower or at least change clothes, and then I just… don’t. I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Sometimes, yeah, I feel low, but a lot of the time it’s more like… blank, or heavy, or like everything is muted. Like I’m just sitting there and I know I’m supposed to feel something more specific, but I can’t really grab onto it.

It’s like I can’t make myself start.\n\nMy mom notices it and she’ll say something like, “Did you shower today?” or “Can you please change?” and I just barely answer her because even explaining why feels exhausting. It’s embarrassing because I know these are basic things, but they feel huge now.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed or shut down like this for at least a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’ve had times before where I was sad or kind of withdrawn, like in high school I’d stay in my room more or not want to talk much, but I don’t know if it was like this. This feels different, or maybe just heavier.\n\nI’m trying to think if there was a clear couple-week period before where I was this shut down, and I honestly don’t know. I don’t remember it being so hard to do basic stuff like showering or changing, not like now. But I also feel like my memory of things is kind of blurry when I look back, so I don’t want to say no absolutely. I just… can’t really point to another time and say, yes, that was the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Like I’m just sitting there and I know I’m supposed to feel something more specific, but I can’t really grab onto it.\n\nIf you mean do I cry all day or feel actively upset the whole day, then no, not really. It’s not like that most of the time. But I also don’t feel okay. I just kind of wake up already tired of the day, and then the day keeps going and I’m still in that same flat, stuck feeling. So… maybe down, but not always sad in a clear way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really. I mean… I don’t even get to the point of doing them most of the time, so it’s hard to tell, but when I think about things I used to like, they don’t pull me in anymore.

Like I used to knit, and it wasn’t some huge thing, but it made me feel calm. I’d pick colors and make little things, and I could sit with it for a while. Now the yarn is just sitting there, and I’ll look at it and think maybe I should do a row or something, and then I just don’t. Same with journaling. I used to write a lot, even if it was messy or just random thoughts, and now opening the notebook feels like too much. Walking too—I used to go outside just to clear my head, but now I’ll stay in my room in the same clothes and the day kind of disappears.

So I guess, no, they don’t feel enjoyable the same way. Or maybe I don’t really give them a chance to feel enjoyable, because I can’t make myself start. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to do something, and I barely answer her because even talking about it feels like a lot. It’s like the things are still there, but the part of me that cared about them is just… not working.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “trying to harm me,” because that sounds dramatic, and I know people react to that. But I have felt like people were watching what I was doing, or like… marking me somehow.

It started more when I was in those college classes. I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.

My mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.

I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.\n\nMy mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe for sure that those emails and comments were sending hidden messages about you?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I mean… I know how that sounds, and I know my mom keeps saying they’re just normal emails or assignment comments, like I’m reading into it too much. But to me it wasn’t just “maybe.” It felt very clear.

It was the wording. Like little changes, certain phrases, the way something would be said but not said directly. Especially with the school portal and the clinic practicum stuff. It felt like they were marking me, like putting this label on me without writing it plainly, like they didn’t want me in healthcare and they were making sure other people knew that too.

So yes, I did believe they were hidden messages about me. I still have a hard time seeing it as just regular feedback, because when I read them, it feels like there’s a second meaning underneath it. My mom can say it’s ordinary, but she wasn’t seeing what I was seeing in the way the words were arranged.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it wasn’t only when I was feeling really shut down. That’s kind of the thing. I know it might seem like maybe I was just in a bad mood and then everything looked darker, but it didn’t feel like that.

Even on days where I wasn’t especially down, like if I was just doing normal school stuff or checking the portal because I had to, I would see the wording in the emails or assignment comments and it would still stand out. The little changes, the careful phrases, the way the clinic practicum people wrote things… it felt like they were using those words to mark me. Like they were saying, without saying it directly, that I wasn’t wanted in healthcare.

My mom would say, “That’s just a regular message,” or “That’s just feedback,” but I didn’t experience it that way. It wasn’t like I had to be crying or hiding in my room for it to happen. Since I was in those college classes, it’s just been something I knew when I saw it. Even if I was otherwise kind of okay that day, the messages still felt like they were about me.

I don’t remember it being so hard to do basic stuff like showering or changing, not like now. But I also feel like my memory of things is kind of blurry when I look back, so I don’t want to say no absolutely. I just… can’t really point to another time and say, yes, that was the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Sometimes, yeah, I feel low, but a lot of the time it’s more like… blank, or heavy, or like everything is muted. Like I’m just sitting there and I know I’m supposed to feel something more specific, but I can’t really grab onto it.\n\nIf you mean do I cry all day or feel actively upset the whole day, then no, not really. It’s not like that most of the time. But I also don’t feel okay. I just kind of wake up already tired of the day, and then the day keeps going and I’m still in that same flat, stuck feeling. So… maybe down, but not always sad in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do things that used to feel enjoyable to you still feel enjoyable when you do them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I don’t even get to the point of doing them most of the time, so it’s hard to tell, but when I think about things I used to like, they don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike I used to knit, and it wasn’t some huge thing, but it made me feel calm. I’d pick colors and make little things, and I could sit with it for a while. Now the yarn is just sitting there, and I’ll look at it and think maybe I should do a row or something, and then I just don’t. Same with journaling. I used to write a lot, even if it was messy or just random thoughts, and now opening the notebook feels like too much. Walking too—I used to go outside just to clear my head, but now I’ll stay in my room in the same clothes and the day kind of disappears.\n\nSo I guess, no, they don’t feel enjoyable the same way. Or maybe I don’t really give them a chance to feel enjoyable, because I can’t make myself start. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to do something, and I barely answer her because even talking about it feels like a lot. It’s like the things are still there, but the part of me that cared about them is just… not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like people are watching you, talking about you, or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “trying to harm me,” because that sounds dramatic, and I know people react to that. But I have felt like people were watching what I was doing, or like… marking me somehow.\n\nIt started more when I was in those college classes. I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.\n\nMy mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for sure that those emails and comments were sending hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I know how that sounds, and I know my mom keeps saying they’re just normal emails or assignment comments, like I’m reading into it too much. But to me it wasn’t just “maybe.” It felt very clear.\n\nIt was the wording. Like little changes, certain phrases, the way something would be said but not said directly. Especially with the school portal and the clinic practicum stuff. It felt like they were marking me, like putting this label on me without writing it plainly, like they didn’t want me in healthcare and they were making sure other people knew that too.\n\nSo yes, I did believe they were hidden messages about me. I still have a hard time seeing it as just regular feedback, because when I read them, it feels like there’s a second meaning underneath it. My mom can say it’s ordinary, but she wasn’t seeing what I was seeing in the way the words were arranged.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed those hidden messages were about you even when you were not feeling down or shut down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it wasn’t only when I was feeling really shut down. That’s kind of the thing. I know it might seem like maybe I was just in a bad mood and then everything looked darker, but it didn’t feel like that.\n\nEven on days where I wasn’t especially down, like if I was just doing normal school stuff or checking the portal because I had to, I would see the wording in the emails or assignment comments and it would still stand out. The little changes, the careful phrases, the way the clinic practicum people wrote things… it felt like they were using those words to mark me. Like they were saying, without saying it directly, that I wasn’t wanted in healthcare.\n\nMy mom would say, “That’s just a regular message,” or “That’s just feedback,” but I didn’t experience it that way. It wasn’t like I had to be crying or hiding in my room for it to happen. Since I was in those college classes, it’s just been something I knew when I saw it. Even if I was otherwise kind of okay that day, the messages still felt like they were about me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And it’s not only showering. I don’t knit anymore, I don’t journal, I don’t really go on walks like I used to. I just kind of stay in my room, and everything feels like too many steps. It’s embarrassing because I know these are basic things, but they feel huge now.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed or shut down like this for at least a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I’ve had times before where I was sad or kind of withdrawn, like in high school I’d stay in my room more or not want to talk much, but I don’t know if it was like this. This feels different, or maybe just heavier.\n\nI’m trying to think if there was a clear couple-week period before where I was this shut down, and I honestly don’t know. I don’t remember it being so hard to do basic stuff like showering or changing, not like now. But I also feel like my memory of things is kind of blurry when I look back, so I don’t want to say no absolutely. I just… can’t really point to another time and say, yes, that was the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. Sometimes, yeah, I feel low, but a lot of the time it’s more like… blank, or heavy, or like everything is muted. Like I’m just sitting there and I know I’m supposed to feel something more specific, but I can’t really grab onto it.\n\nIf you mean do I cry all day or feel actively upset the whole day, then no, not really. It’s not like that most of the time. But I also don’t feel okay. I just kind of wake up already tired of the day, and then the day keeps going and I’m still in that same flat, stuck feeling. So… maybe down, but not always sad in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do things that used to feel enjoyable to you still feel enjoyable when you do them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I don’t even get to the point of doing them most of the time, so it’s hard to tell, but when I think about things I used to like, they don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike I used to knit, and it wasn’t some huge thing, but it made me feel calm. I’d pick colors and make little things, and I could sit with it for a while. Now the yarn is just sitting there, and I’ll look at it and think maybe I should do a row or something, and then I just don’t. Same with journaling. I used to write a lot, even if it was messy or just random thoughts, and now opening the notebook feels like too much. Walking too—I used to go outside just to clear my head, but now I’ll stay in my room in the same clothes and the day kind of disappears.\n\nSo I guess, no, they don’t feel enjoyable the same way. Or maybe I don’t really give them a chance to feel enjoyable, because I can’t make myself start. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to do something, and I barely answer her because even talking about it feels like a lot. It’s like the things are still there, but the part of me that cared about them is just… not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like people are watching you, talking about you, or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “trying to harm me,” because that sounds dramatic, and I know people react to that. But I have felt like people were watching what I was doing, or like… marking me somehow.\n\nIt started more when I was in those college classes. I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.\n\nMy mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for sure that those emails and comments were sending hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I know how that sounds, and I know my mom keeps saying they’re just normal emails or assignment comments, like I’m reading into it too much. But to me it wasn’t just “maybe.” It felt very clear.\n\nIt was the wording. Like little changes, certain phrases, the way something would be said but not said directly. Especially with the school portal and the clinic practicum stuff. It felt like they were marking me, like putting this label on me without writing it plainly, like they didn’t want me in healthcare and they were making sure other people knew that too.\n\nSo yes, I did believe they were hidden messages about me. I still have a hard time seeing it as just regular feedback, because when I read them, it feels like there’s a second meaning underneath it. My mom can say it’s ordinary, but she wasn’t seeing what I was seeing in the way the words were arranged.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed those hidden messages were about you even when you were not feeling down or shut down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it wasn’t only when I was feeling really shut down. That’s kind of the thing. I know it might seem like maybe I was just in a bad mood and then everything looked darker, but it didn’t feel like that.\n\nEven on days where I wasn’t especially down, like if I was just doing normal school stuff or checking the portal because I had to, I would see the wording in the emails or assignment comments and it would still stand out. The little changes, the careful phrases, the way the clinic practicum people wrote things… it felt like they were using those words to mark me. Like they were saying, without saying it directly, that I wasn’t wanted in healthcare.\n\nMy mom would say, “That’s just a regular message,” or “That’s just feedback,” but I didn’t experience it that way. It wasn’t like I had to be crying or hiding in my room for it to happen. Since I was in those college classes, it’s just been something I knew when I saw it. Even if I was otherwise kind of okay that day, the messages still felt like they were about me.\n\nDoctor: Are these hidden messages all part of one clear belief about what people are doing to you, or does it feel more jumbled and hard to make sense of?\n\nPatient: It feels like one clear thing, mostly. Like, it’s not just random words jumping out with no connection. It’s all connected to the same idea, that since I was in those college classes, especially with the clinic practicum, they were using the school portal and the emails and the assignment comments to mark me somehow.\n\nLike the wording changes weren’t just… weird by themselves. They fit together. A phrase would be slightly different, or feedback would be written in this careful way, and it felt like the point was always the same: that I’m not wanted in healthcare, or that I’m being labeled as someone who shouldn’t be there. That’s why it’s hard when my mom says it’s just regular school messages, because to her they’re separate normal emails, but to me they’re part of the same pattern.\n\nI mean, I guess it can feel confusing because they don’t say it directly. That’s the part that makes me go over it again and again, because it’s hidden under normal wording. But the meaning feels clear to me. It’s not like a bunch of unrelated things. It’s more like all those little wording choices are pointing to the same thing about me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your body would stay frozen or in one position for a long time, even when you wanted to move?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, at least. I mean, I do sit still for a long time sometimes, especially if I’m reading something over and over or trying to figure out what something means, but I wouldn’t say my body is frozen in one position and I can’t move even though I want to.\n\nLike, I might not get up right away, or I might feel kind of stuck because I’m focused or overwhelmed, but if I decide to move, I can usually move. I haven’t really noticed my arms or legs staying in one position on their own or anything like that. If you mean literally frozen, then no, I don’t think that’s been happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

A phrase would be slightly different, or feedback would be written in this careful way, and it felt like the point was always the same: that I’m not wanted in healthcare, or that I’m being labeled as someone who shouldn’t be there. That’s why it’s hard when my mom says it’s just regular school messages, because to her they’re separate normal emails, but to me they’re part of the same pattern.\n\nI mean, I guess it can feel confusing because they don’t say it directly. That’s the part that makes me go over it again and again, because it’s hidden under normal wording. But the meaning feels clear to me. It’s not like a bunch of unrelated things. It’s more like all those little wording choices are pointing to the same thing about me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your body would stay frozen or in one position for a long time, even when you wanted to move?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, at least. I mean, I do sit still for a long time sometimes, especially if I’m reading something over and over or trying to figure out what something means, but I wouldn’t say my body is frozen in one position and I can’t move even though I want to.\n\nLike, I might not get up right away, or I might feel kind of stuck because I’m focused or overwhelmed, but if I decide to move, I can usually move. I haven’t really noticed my arms or legs staying in one position on their own or anything like that. If you mean literally frozen, then no, I don’t think that’s been happening.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo people ever tell you that your speech is hard to follow or that your thoughts seem mixed up?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I don’t really know. I mean, my mom sometimes tells me I’m “going in circles” or that I’m making things too complicated, but I don’t think people usually say my actual speech is hard to follow.

Like, when I try to explain what I mean, especially if I’m trying to be really exact, I can end up saying a lot because I don’t want someone to misunderstand me. Then I’ll back up and explain the context, and then explain why I’m explaining it, and I can tell people get impatient. But I don’t know if that means my thoughts are mixed up, or if they just don’t want to listen to the whole thing.

I don’t think I’m saying random things or anything. In my head it connects. It just takes me a while to get it out the right way. Sometimes people act like I’m overexplaining, but I’m trying to make it clear, not confusing. So… maybe they’ve said I talk too much or get stuck on details, but not exactly that they can’t follow me.

Now I just… don’t. I think about doing them and then it feels like there’s this huge wall between me and actually moving. My mom asks me what’s wrong or tries to get me to talk, and I barely answer because even answering feels like too much effort. It’s embarrassing, honestly. Sometimes, yeah, I feel low, but a lot of the time it’s more like… blank, or heavy, or like everything is muted. It’s not like that most of the time. But I also don’t feel okay. I just kind of wake up already tired of the day, and then the day keeps going and I’m still in that same flat, stuck feeling. So… maybe down, but not always sad in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Do things that used to feel enjoyable to you still feel enjoyable when you do them?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I don’t even get to the point of doing them most of the time, so it’s hard to tell, but when I think about things I used to like, they don’t pull me in anymore.\n\nLike I used to knit, and it wasn’t some huge thing, but it made me feel calm. I’d pick colors and make little things, and I could sit with it for a while. Now the yarn is just sitting there, and I’ll look at it and think maybe I should do a row or something, and then I just don’t. Same with journaling. I used to write a lot, even if it was messy or just random thoughts, and now opening the notebook feels like too much. Walking too—I used to go outside just to clear my head, but now I’ll stay in my room in the same clothes and the day kind of disappears.\n\nSo I guess, no, they don’t feel enjoyable the same way. Or maybe I don’t really give them a chance to feel enjoyable, because I can’t make myself start. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to do something, and I barely answer her because even talking about it feels like a lot. It’s like the things are still there, but the part of me that cared about them is just… not working.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like people are watching you, talking about you, or trying to harm you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “trying to harm me,” because that sounds dramatic, and I know people react to that. But I have felt like people were watching what I was doing, or like… marking me somehow.\n\nIt started more when I was in those college classes. I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.\n\nMy mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe for sure that those emails and comments were sending hidden messages about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I know how that sounds, and I know my mom keeps saying they’re just normal emails or assignment comments, like I’m reading into it too much. But to me it wasn’t just “maybe.” It felt very clear.\n\nIt was the wording. Like little changes, certain phrases, the way something would be said but not said directly. Especially with the school portal and the clinic practicum stuff. I still have a hard time seeing it as just regular feedback, because when I read them, it feels like there’s a second meaning underneath it. My mom can say it’s ordinary, but she wasn’t seeing what I was seeing in the way the words were arranged.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you believed those hidden messages were about you even when you were not feeling down or shut down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it wasn’t only when I was feeling really shut down. The little changes, the careful phrases, the way the clinic practicum people wrote things… it felt like they were using those words to mark me. Like they were saying, without saying it directly, that I wasn’t wanted in healthcare.\n\nMy mom would say, “That’s just a regular message,” or “That’s just feedback,” but I didn’t experience it that way. It wasn’t like I had to be crying or hiding in my room for it to happen. Since I was in those college classes, it’s just been something I knew when I saw it. Even if I was otherwise kind of okay that day, the messages still felt like they were about me.\n\nDoctor: Are these hidden messages all part of one clear belief about what people are doing to you, or does it feel more jumbled and hard to make sense of?\n\nPatient: It feels like one clear thing, mostly. I’m not sure. I don’t think of what I do as disorganized, exactly. Other people might think it doesn’t make sense because they don’t have the whole context in their head the way I do.

Like, sometimes my mom will say, “Why are you doing that?” if I’m checking something again or if I’m trying to compare wording from one thing to another, and I guess from the outside it probably looks like I’m just wasting time or acting weird. But to me there’s a reason I’m doing it. I’m trying to understand what something means, or make sure I didn’t miss something.

I don’t think I’ve done things like… I don’t know, completely random stuff with no purpose. I’m not aware of that. I can get really caught up in something and then maybe I don’t do things in the order people expect, or I take a long time, or I seem distracted. But in my mind it’s not random. It feels like I’m following a reason, even if other people don’t agree with the reason.

So I guess people may have thought my behavior was strange or frustrating, but I don’t know if I’d call it disorganized. I’d need to know what you mean by that.

Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. This feels different, or maybe just heavier.\n\nI’m trying to think if there was a clear couple-week period before where I was this shut down, and I honestly don’t know. I don’t remember it being so hard to do basic stuff like showering or changing, not like now.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. I used to write a lot, even if it was messy or just random thoughts, and now opening the notebook feels like too much. Walking too—I used to go outside just to clear my head, but now I’ll stay in my room in the same clothes and the day kind of disappears.\n\nSo I guess, no, they don’t feel enjoyable the same way. Or maybe I don’t really give them a chance to feel enjoyable, because I can’t make myself start. My mom will ask what’s wrong or try to get me to do something, and I barely answer her because even talking about it feels like a lot. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… I mean, I don’t usually say it like “trying to harm me,” because that sounds dramatic, and I know people react to that. But I have felt like people were watching what I was doing, or like… marking me somehow.\n\nIt started more when I was in those college classes. I’d look at the school portal and the emails and the comments on assignments, and there would be these little wording changes. Like a phrase would be slightly different, or something would be worded in this careful way, and it felt like it was meant to signal something about me. Especially with the clinic practicum people. It felt like they were making it clear that they didn’t want me in healthcare, but not saying it directly where I could point to it.\n\nMy mom says they’re just regular messages, like normal school emails or feedback, and I get that that’s what she thinks. But when I read them, it doesn’t feel normal. It feels like there’s another layer underneath it, like they’re using certain words to tag me as someone who shouldn’t be there. So… yeah, I guess I do feel talked about or watched in that way. Not like someone is standing outside my window or anything, but like through the portal and the clinic stuff, like they all know and they’re communicating it without saying it openly.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so… not like that, at least. I mean, I do sit still for a long time sometimes, especially if I’m reading something over and over or trying to figure out what something means, but I wouldn’t say my body is frozen in one position and I can’t move even though I want to.\n\nLike, I might not get up right away, or I might feel kind of stuck because I’m focused or overwhelmed, but if I decide to move, I can usually move. I haven’t really noticed my arms or legs staying in one position on their own or anything like that. If you mean literally frozen, then no, I don’t think that’s been happening.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
